### Assistant

_source line 1088 · 2026-05-15T16:43:21.140Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This confirms it. MATH-500 DS importance at s=0.25 shows rich, diverse ii_low values: 18.38, 1.95, 0.80, 1.73, 1.94, -0.24, 20.65, 4.49, ... — genuine non-zero floating-point values. Many are quite large (70.59, 1524.13, 87.52, 88.06, etc.).

But GSM8K DS importance has ii_low = exactly 0.0 for ALL non-null entries across ALL sparsity levels.
 
This stark difference is troubling. MATH-500 clearly shows that importance-based masking creates compound interactions in the low-sparsity phase, yet GSM8K shows none at all. Either there's a bug where the mask isn't being applied correctly for GSM8K's low-sparsity segments, or GSM8K's attention patterns are fundamentally different in a way that makes importance pruning completely benign during that phase.

The exact 0.0 values across the board point toward a bug—getting perfect zeros in floating-point computation across hundreds of paths is nearly impossible unless the computation is actually a no-op. I'm examining the compute_conditioned_perplexities function and how it applies the importance mask to per-segment token indices...

Checking the II computation logic...

The attention head mask and KV mask are computed once per sparsity level outside the phase loop, so they're global across all phases. When compute_conditioned_perplexities runs on specific phase tokens under these masks, if the importance ranking only selects heads active in higher sparsity phases, the mask would have no effect on low-sparsity tokens—making the masked PPL identical to dense PPL and yielding II = 0.

But MATH-500 produces non-zero II_low with the same model and masking approach, so the real question is why the importance rankings diverge between these datasets...

Actually, the importance scores are computed per-problem from that specific input's attention patterns, so the ranking naturally differs across problems. Longer traces in MATH-500 might create attention distributions where even low-sparsity phase tokens have some masked heads, while GSM8K's shorter traces could produce different distributions where the importance ranking cleanly separates low-sparsity from high-sparsity heads. Though achieving perfect separation across all 50 problems and 4 sparsity levels seems statistically implausible for real data.

I need to trace through the code more carefully to understand how head_importance is actually computed. Looking at the initialization around line 357-358, these values start as None for random masking, but for importance masking they should be set to actual values somewhere.
```

</details>

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
Now I can see the key section. Let me look at the importance masking code more carefully.

Lines around 340-360:
```python
if use_importance:
    dense_ce_losses, head_importance, kv_importance = (
        ii_calc.compute_dense_forward_with_importance(full_ids)
    )
else:
    dense_ce_losses = ii_calc.compute_dense_ce_losses(full_ids)


```

When importance masking isn't being used, I'm setting those importance values to None, then extracting the sequence length and iterating through the annotation phases to collect token and segment counts. I'm also getting the model dimensions to determine how many layers and attention heads we're working with, then looping through each sparsity level to create attention head masks based on the importance scores.

Now I'm computing the KV mask for each sparsity level by selecting which key-value positions to evict based on their importance scores. The masking happens globally across the full sequence rather than per-phase, so the head importance comes from a dense forward pass over everything, and the KV importance is position-level. This means positions with high cumulative attention in low-sparsity phases would have higher importance and survive eviction, which could explain why KV perplexity matches dense perplexity.

The same logic applies to head masking — if the globally least important heads are inactive during low-sparsity phases, masking them wouldn't affect those segments' perplexity. But getting exactly 0.0 difference would require the masked heads to contribute absolutely nothing to the residual stream for those positions, which is nearly impossible in floating-point unless the mask doesn't actually remove any heads with non-zero contributions.

Let me check the `compute_conditioned_perplexities` function to see how it actually computes perplexity when applying both the global head mask and position-level KV mask to specific phase segments.

If it's doing a forward pass with binary 0/1 masking that zeroes out head outputs, then masked heads contribute nothing and perplexity should differ from the dense baseline. But MATH-500 shows non-zero improvement from importance masking on low-sparsity phases, while GSM8K shows zero — suggesting either a different masking implementation between the two datasets or something about GSM8K's attention patterns that makes importance masking ineffective on those segments.

I should compare how the two experiments differ — MATH-500 uses the original profiling script while GSM8K appears to use a different one.
````

</details>

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

````text
The `compute_conditioned_perplexities` functions are identical between the GSM8K and MATH-500 scripts. Both call `ii_calc.forward_with_sparse_config()` with the same interface.

Now I need to check one critical detail: the phase annotation. Looking at the GSM8K script:

```python
for phase in annotation.phases:
    phase.start += prompt_len
    phase.end += prompt_len
```

This shifts the phase token indices by prompt_len. This is important because the generation starts after the prompt, but the full_ids include both prompt and generation. 

The phase segmentation is based on generation-only tokens, so the indices need to be shifted to align with the full sequence. In the MATH-500 script, I should verify if the same shifting is applied. Looking at the GSM8K implementation, the phase annotation is created from gen_sparsity (generation-only tokens), then shifted by prompt_len so that `phase.token_indices` reference the correct positions in full_ids. But I'm noticing a potential issue: the `attn_head_mask` is computed globally rather than per-position, so it affects all positions equally, whereas `kv_mask` is position-specific and needs careful handling.

The `kv_importance` metric measures cumulative attention received, so tokens with high importance survive KV eviction. In the low-sparsity phase where attention is concentrated, the tokens receiving that concentrated attention have very high importance and are preserved. This same logic should apply to MATH-500, where ii_low was non-zero under importance masking. Let me compare what's actually different between the two datasets.

GSM8K has shorter sequences and simpler math problems that might use more uniform attention patterns compared to MATH-500. The exact 0.0 result suggests the masked forward passes are producing identical perplexity to the dense baseline, which would only happen if the sparsity ratios are all 1.0 — meaning no actual compression is occurring. I need to check the `forward_with_sparse_config` function to see if there's a bug preventing the masking from being applied correctly.

For this to happen, the pruned attention heads would need to contribute zero weight to predicting low-sparsity tokens, and the evicted KV entries would need to never be attended to by those same tokens. If both conditions hold across all 50 problems and all 4 sparsity levels simultaneously, that would explain why all three masking conditions match the dense baseline.

But at s=0.30, you're removing 30% of heads and 30% of KV entries globally—it's implausible that these pruned components have absolutely zero impact on low-sparsity token prediction across every problem. This suggests there might be a bug in how the sparse configuration is being applied or how the loss is being computed over the specified token positions.

The masks are applied globally throughout the forward pass, affecting hidden states at all positions through residual connections and layer normalization, even if the measured loss is only computed at certain token indices. So the question becomes whether the KV eviction is actually removing the least-attended positions, or if there's something else going on with how attention is being calculated and which positions are being ranked for importance.

Head masking works differently than KV eviction—when a head is masked to zero, it zeros out its output across all positions, which should propagate through the residual connections and affect subsequent layers. The only way this could have zero effect on the low-sparsity positions is if those masked heads were already producing zero attention weights for those specific positions, which seems statistically unlikely across all heads and positions.

Let me check what the actual `_HeadMaskHook` implementation does to understand how head masking is applied in this codebase.
````

{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"stdout\": \"    def forward_with_sparse_config(\\n        self,\\n        input_ids: torch.Tensor,\\n        attention_mask: Optional[torch.Tensor] = None,\\n        attn_head_mask: Optional[torch.Tensor] = None,\\n        kv_mask: Optional[torch.Tensor] = None,\\n        phase_token_indices: Optional[List[int]] = None,\\n    ) -> float:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Run a forward pass under a given sparsity config, return mean perplexity.\\n\\n        Args:\\n            input_ids: (batch, seq_len).\\n            attention_mask: (batch, seq_len), 1=attend. Defaults to all-ones.\\n            attn_head_mask: (num_layers, num_heads), 1=active 0=masked.\\n            kv_mask: (num_layers, seq_len), 1=keep 0=evict.\\n            phase_token_indices: Positions at which to evaluate perplexity.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        input_ids = input_ids.to(self.device)\\n        if attention_mask is None:\\n            attention_mask = torch.ones_like(input_ids)\\n        attention_mask = attention_mask.to(self.device)\\n\\n        # --- KV eviction: fold into attention_mask ---\\n        model_attention_mask = attention_mask\\n        if kv_mask is not None:\\n            kv_mask = kv_mask.to(self.device)\\n            if kv_mask.dim() == 2:\\n                flat_kv_mask = kv_mask.min(dim=0).values\\n            else:\\n                flat_kv_mask = kv_mask\\n            model_attention_mask = attention_mask * flat_kv_mask.unsqueeze(0)\\n\\n        # --- Head masking: install hooks ---\\n        hook_ctx: Optional[_HeadMaskHook] = None\\n        if attn_head_mask is not None:\\n            hook_ctx = _HeadMaskHook(self.model, attn_head_mask)\\n\\n        try:\\n            outputs = self.model(\\n                input_ids=input_ids,\\n                attention_mask=model_attention_mask,\\n            )\\n        finally:\\n            if hook_ctx is not None:\\n                hook_ctx.remove()\\n\\n        logits = outputs.logits[0]   # (seq_len, vocab)\\n        labels = input_ids[0]        # (seq_len,)\\n\\n        return compute_perplexity(logits, labels, token_indices=phase_token_indices)\\n\\n    # ------------------------------------------------------------------\\n    # Mask construction helpers\\n    # ------------------------------------------------------------------\\n\\n    @staticmethod\\n    def _select_heads_to_mask(\\n        num_layers: int,\\n        num_heads: int,\\n        ratio: float,\\n        importance: Optional[torch.Tensor] = None,\\n    ) -> torch.Tensor:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Build a (num_layers, num_heads) binary mask, masking `ratio` of heads.\\n\\n        If importance is given (num_layers, num_heads), mask the least important.\\n        Otherwise, mask randomly.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        mask = torch.ones(num_layers, num_heads)\\n        num_to_mask = max(1, int(num_heads * ratio))\\n\\n        for layer in range(num_layers):\\n            if importance is not None:\\n                _, indices = importance[layer].topk(num_to_mask, largest=False)\\n            else:\\n                indices = torch.randperm(num_heads)[:num_to_mask]\\n            mask[layer, indices] = 0.0\\n\\n        return mask\\n\\n    @staticmethod\\n    def _select_kv_to_evict(\\n        seq_len: int,\\n        ratio: float,\\n        importance: Optional[torch.Tensor] = None,\\n    ) -> torch.Tensor:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Build a (seq_len,) binary mask shared across all layers, evicting `ratio` of KV positions.\\n\\n        If importance is given ((seq_len,) or (num_layers, seq_len)), evict the least important.\\n        Otherwise, evict randomly. Position 0 (BOS) is never evicted.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        mask = torch.ones(seq_len)\\n        num_to_evict = max(1, int(seq_len * ratio))\\n        num_to_evict = min(num_to_evict, seq_len - 1)\\n\\n        if importance is not None:\\n            if importance.dim() == 2:\\n                importance = importance.mean(dim=0)\\n            seq_len_imp = importance.shape[0]\\n            position_counts = torch.arange(seq_len_imp, 0, -1, device=importance.device, dtype=importance.dtype)\\n            importance = importance / position_counts\\n            candidates = importance[1:]\\n            _, indices = candidates.topk(num_to_evict,\\n\\n... kv_importance\\n        )\\n\\n        ppl_dense = self.forward_with_sparse_config(\\n            input_ids, phase_token_indices=phase_token_indices,\\n        )\\n        ppl_attn = self.forward_with_sparse_config(\\n            input_ids, attn_head_mask=attn_head_mask,\\n            phase_token_indices=phase_token_indices,\\n        )\\n        ppl_kv = self.forward_with_sparse_config(\\n            input_ids, kv_mask=kv_mask,\\n            phase_token_indices=phase_token_indices,\\n        )\\n        ppl_both = self.forward_with_sparse_config(\\n            input_ids, attn_head_mask=attn_head_mask, kv_mask=kv_mask,\\n            phase_token_indices=phase_token_indices,\\n        )\\n\\n        r_attn = ppl_attn / ppl_dense if ppl_dense > 0 else float(\\\"nan\\\")\\n        r_kv = ppl_kv / ppl_dense if ppl_dense > 0 else float(\\\"nan\\\")\\n        r_both = ppl_both / ppl_dense if ppl_dense > 0 else float(\\\"nan\\\")\\n        ii = r_both - r_attn - r_kv + 1\\n\\n        num_tokens = len(phase_token_indices) if phase_token_indices else seq_len\\n\\n        return InteractionResult(\\n            ii_value=ii,\\n            ppl_dense=ppl_dense,\\n            ppl_attn_sparse=ppl_attn,\\n            ppl_kv_sparse=ppl_kv,\\n            ppl_both_sparse=ppl_both,\\n            r_attn=r_attn,\\n            r_kv=r_kv,\\n            r_both=r_both,\\n            is_super_additive=ii > 0,\\n            sparsity_level=sparsity_level,\\n            num_tokens=num_tokens,\\n        )\\n\\n    # ------------------------------------------------------------------\\n    # Per-phase Interaction Index\\n    # ------------------------------------------------------------------\\n\\n    def compute_per_phase_ii(\\n        self,\\n        input_ids: torch.Tensor,\\n        phases: List,\\n        sparsity_level: float = 0.3,\\n    ) -> Dict[str, InteractionResult]:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Compute II separately for each phase (reasoning / execution / transition).\\n\\n        Args:\\n            phases: List of Phase objects with .name and .token_indices attributes.\\n            sparsity_level: Fraction of heads to mask / KV positions to evict.\\n\\n        Returns:\\n            Dict mapping phase name to its InteractionResult.\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        results: Dict[str, InteractionResult] = {}\\n\\n        for phase in phases:\\n            result = self.compute_interaction_index(\\n                input_ids,\\n                sparsity_level=sparsity_level,\\n                phase_token_indices=phase.token_indices,\\n            )\\n            result.phase_name = phase.name\\n            results[phase.name] = result\\n\\n        return results\\n\\n\\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n# Bootstrap CI for Interaction Index\\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n\\ndef bootstrap_interaction_index(\\n    ii_samples: List[float],\\n    n_resamples: int = 1000,\\n    ci_level: float = 0.95,\\n    seed: int = 42,\\n) -> Dict:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Bootstrap confidence intervals for mean and median II.\\n\\n    Returns dict with mean/ci_low/ci_high (mean-based) and\\n    median/median_ci_low/median_ci_high (median-based, primary).\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)\\n    samples = np.array(ii_samples)\\n    n = len(samples)\\n    boot_means = np.empty(n_resamples)\\n    boot_medians = np.empty(n_resamples)\\n    for i in range(n_resamples):\\n        idx = rng.randint(0, n, size=n)\\n        boot_means[i] = samples[idx].mean()\\n        boot_medians[i] = np.median(samples[idx])\\n    alpha = 1.0 - ci_level\\n    lo_pct = 100 * alpha / 2\\n    hi_pct = 100 * (1 - alpha / 2)\\n    return {\\n        \\\"mean\\\": float(samples.mean()),\\n        \\\"ci_low\\\": float(np.percentile(boot_means, lo_pct)),\\n        \\\"ci_high\\\": float(np.percentile(boot_means, hi_pct)),\\n        \\\"median\\\": float(np.median(samples)),\\n        \\\"median_ci_low\\\": float(np.percentile(boot_medians, lo_pct)),\\n        \\\"median_ci_high\\\": float(np.percentile(boot_medians, hi_pct)),\\n    }\\n\", Now I can see the complete picture. Let me analyze the GSM8K II_low = 0.0 issue more carefully.

Key code in `forward_with_sparse_config`:

```python
# --- KV eviction: fold into attention_mask ---
model_attention_mask = attention_mask
if kv_mask is not None:
    kv_mask = kv_mask.to(self.device)
    if kv_mask.dim() == 2:
        flat_kv_mask = kv_mask.min(dim=0).values
    else:
        flat_kv_mask = kv_mask
    model_attention_mask = attention_mask * flat_kv_mask.unsqueeze(0)

# --- Head masking: install hooks ---
hook_ctx: Optional[_HeadMaskHook] = None
if attn_head_mask is not None:
    hook_ctx = _HeadMaskHook(self.model, attn_head_mask)

try:
    outputs = self.model(
        input_ids=input_ids,
        attention_mask=model_attention_mask,
    )
finally:
    if hook_ctx is not None:
        hook_ctx.remove()
```

I'm flattening the KV mask and combining it with the attention mask, then setting up head masking hooks before running the model forward pass.

Now looking at the `_select_kv_to_evict` function, I'm calculating how many tokens to evict based on a ratio, then if importance scores are provided, I'm normalizing them by position and selecting the top candidates to evict while excluding the BOS token. The KV mask itself is a 1D tensor shared across the sequence...

The eviction strategy prioritizes removing positions that receive the least cumulative attention relative to how many tokens could have attended to them—later positions are naturally penalized since fewer tokens came after them. When low-sparsity tokens concentrate their attention on specific positions, those positions get high importance scores and survive eviction, even though other positions might still receive some attention from them, just less than from the broader token set.

For head masking, I'm identifying the globally least important heads per layer based on their attention Frobenius norm, and these heads tend to contribute minimally to the positions that low-sparsity tokens focus on. The core insight is that this importance-based masking naturally avoids disrupting the components that concentrated-attention tokens rely on.

On MATH-500 with longer sequences, there's enough diversity in which heads and positions matter that even importance-based selection ends up masking some components used by low-sparsity positions. But on GSM8K with shorter sequences, the attention concentration is tighter, so importance-based selection might perfectly sidestep all components those positions depend on. The fact that I'm seeing exactly 0.0 across all 50 problems, all sparsity levels, and every computable entry is suspicious—even floating-point rounding would typically leave something like 1e-10. The only way to get perfect zeros is if the model produces identical logits regardless of which attention components are masked.

But wait, the dense PPL comes from cached losses while the masked PPLs run through a fresh forward pass, so there should be some numerical drift from GPU non-determinism alone. Unless the masking actually creates identical computation paths. If the head mask is all 1s, it multiplies by 1.0 and changes nothing, but I know heads are being masked—at s=0.15 for instance, the least important heads per layer get masked. Let me trace through exactly what's happening with the head selection logic.

(num_heads,)\\n            handle = attn_module.register_forward_hook(\\n                self._make_hook(mask)\\n            )\\n            self.handles.append(handle)\\n\\n    @staticmethod\\n    def _find_attention_layers(model: torch.nn.Module) -> list:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Heuristic: find all self-attention sub-modules by common naming.\\\"\\\"\\\"\\n        layers = []\\n        for name, module in model.named_modules():\\n            # self.forward_with_sparse_config(\\n            input_ids, kv_mask=kv_mask,\\n            phase_token_indices=phase_token_indices,\\n        )\\n        ppl_both = self.forward_with_sparse_config(\\n r_attn - r_kv + 1\\n\\n        num_tokens = len(phase_token_indices) if phase_token_indices else seq_len\\n\\n        return InteractionResult(\\n            ii_value=ii,\\n            ppl_dense=ppl_dense,\\n            ppl_attn_sparse=ppl_attn,\\n            ppl_kv_sparse=ppl_kv,\\n            ppl_both_sparse=ppl_both,\\n            r_attn=r_attn,\\n            r_kv=r_kv,\\n compute_per_phase_ii(\\n        self,\\n        input_ids: torch.Tensor,\\n        phases: List,\\n        sparsity_level: float = 0.3,\\n    ) -> Dict[str, InteractionResult]:\\n        \\\"\\\"\\\"Compute II separately for each phase (reasoning / execution / transition).\\n\\n        Args:\\n            phases: List of Phase objects with .name and .token_indices attributes.\\n Dict[str, InteractionResult] = {}\\n\\n        for phase in phases:\\n            result = self.compute_interaction_index(\\n                input_ids,\\n                sparsity_level=sparsity_level,\\n                phase_token_indices=phase.token_indices,\\n            )\\n            result.phase_name = phase.name\\n            results[phase.name] = result\\n\\n        return results\\n\\n\\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n# Bootstrap CI for Interaction Index\\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n\\ndef bootstrap_interaction_index(\\n    ii_samples: List[float],\\n    n_resamples: int = 1000,\\n    ci_level: float = 0.95,\\n    seed: int = 42,\\n) -> Dict:\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"Bootstrap confidence intervals for mean and median II.\\n\\n    Returns dict with mean/ci_low/ci_high (mean-based) and\\n    median/median_ci_low/median_ci_high (median-based, primary).\\n    \\\"\\\"\\\"\\n    rng = np.random.RandomState(seed)\\n    samples = np.array(ii_samples)\\n    n = len(samples)\\n    boot_means = np.empty(n_resamples)\\n    boot_medians = np.empty(n_resamples)\\n    for i in range(n_resamples):\\n        idx = rng.randint(0, n, size=n)\\n        boot_means[i] = samples[idx].mean()\\n        boot_medians[i] = np.median(samples[idx])\\n    alpha = 1.0 - ci_level\\n    lo_pct = 100 * alpha / 2\\n    hi_pct = 100 * (1 - alpha / 2)\\n    return {\\n        \\\"mean\\\": float(samples.mean()),\\n        \\\"ci_low\\\": float(np.percentile(boot_means, lo_pct)),\\n        \\\"ci_high\\\": float(np.percentile(boot_means, hi_pct)),\\n        \\\"median\\\": _source line 1131 · 2026-05-15T16:46:17.011Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the complete picture. The `_HeadMaskHook` zeros out masked heads at the attention layer output level. This is a proper intervention — it changes the hidden states for ALL sequence positions. Even if a head has zero attention weight at a specific position, zeroing the head output removes its contribution to the residual stream (which includes the value projection weighted by attention).

The fact that `forward_with_sparse_config` does a FULL forward pass (not a cached one) for the masked conditions means we get fresh logits under the masked configuration. The dense PPL is from cached losses (`ppl_from_cached_losses`), while masked PPLs are from fresh forward passes.
 
But here's what's bothering me: if we're getting II = 0.0 exactly, that means `ppl_attn = ppl_kv = ppl_both = ppl_dense` exactly. The dense PPL comes from cached losses in a single forward pass, while the masked PPLs come from separate fresh forward passes. Even without any masking effect, floating-point non-determinism between different forward passes should introduce small differences — typically in the range of 1e-7 to 1e-5 relative error. Getting exact equality is suspicious and suggests there might be a code path that's bypassing the actual computation somehow.

Looking at the GSM8K implementation, I see the dense PPL is calculated using cached losses from an earlier forward pass, while the masked variants use a different computation path entirely. These should produce slightly different numerical results even if the masks don't affect anything. The fact that they're coming out identical makes me wonder if there's something in the code that's taking a shortcut when the masks are all ones. , running the same input through the same model twice on the same GPU often produces identical results when the computation graph and CUDA kernels remain consistent and there are no stochastic operations involved. So it's plausible that two forward passes yield the same output, and if the mask has negligible effect on those positions, the masked pass would match too. But this still doesn't explain why DS importance shows this behavior on GSM8K while MATH-500 exhibits different patterns—on MATH-500 the longer traces and diverse attention patterns mean masked heads still contribute meaningfully to low-sparsity positions, whereas GSM8K might have more uniform or simpler attention structures where masking truly has zero impact.

The probability calculation is striking though—if roughly 100 non-null entries each have even a 5% chance of being non-zero, getting all zeros is vanishingly unlikely, which suggests something systematic is happening rather than random chance.

Looking at the code, I see that importance is computed once per problem before the sparsity loop, so the ranking stays constant while only the masking threshold changes at different sparsity levels. This means at higher sparsity like 0.15, we're masking the bottom 15% of heads according to that fixed importance ranking, and if those heads truly have zero effect on the loss, that would explain the pattern.

But at 0.30 sparsity, we're masking the bottom 30%, which includes those original 15% plus an additional 15% of heads. For the zero-effect pattern to hold, this second tier of heads would also need to contribute nothing to the low-sparsity tokens—a much stricter constraint that makes the genuine zero hypothesis less plausible. I should check whether there's a bug in how importance scores are being computed, or whether the importance calculation is even properly hooking into the attention layers for this specific model.

Actually, looking at the MATH-500 profiling results for DeepSeek, the same `compute_dense_forward_with_importance` function produces valid non-zero importance scores there, so the issue seems specific to the GSM8K dataset rather than a fundamental model compatibility problem.

I think I have enough to summarize my findings now. The main issues are: the aggregate perplexity reporting is broken (showing NaN), but the per-problem importance values seem to be computed correctly from segment-level ratios. The real puzzle is why DeepSeek's importance scores are exactly zero across all non-null entries on GSM8K when MATH-500 shows rich variation—this could be a data-specific phenomenon or point to something else I haven't fully investigated yet.

The DeepSeek importance verdict passes trivially because zero values don't overlap with the confidence interval, which isn't meaningful evidence of phase modulation. Meanwhile, the random masking verdict fails because the low and mid confidence intervals do overlap, suggesting GSM8K problems might not exhibit as strong phase differentiation as MATH-500 does. Both conditions also have a significant portion of null entries where importance scores couldn't be computed.

The mid-sparsity importance scores are suspiciously large—an order of magnitude higher than what I'm seeing on MATH-500—which points to either outliers skewing the means or a deeper computation issue. Given that only about half the problems have valid low-sparsity data, the effective sample size is too small to draw reliable conclusions. I need to add diagnostic logging to check whether the masking is actually changing perplexity for low-sparsity tokens, and probably run a smaller validation set first to isolate where the problem is.
```

  "text": "## 审查报告 — gsm8k_profiling_ds (DS DeepSeek GSM8K cross-validation)\n\n**结论**: CRITICAL — DS importance 数据退化，DS random FAIL，结果不可用于 cross-dataset 泛化声明\n\n### 发现\n\n**1. [severity: critical] DS importance II_low = 0.0 完全退化**\n\nDS importance 的 per-problem ii_low 数据：200 条中 100 条 = null，100 条 = 精确 0.0，**零条非零值**。\n\n对比 MATH-500 DS importance（s=0.25 同一模型）：ii_low 值范围 -5.4 到 1524，分布丰富。\n\n这两组数据来自同一模型、同一代码路径（InteractionIndexCalculator.forward_with_sparse_config）。GSM8K 全部精确 0.0 意味着 ppl_attn = ppl_kv = ppl_both = ppl_dense（浮点完全一致），即 importance masking 对 low-sparsity phase tokens 完全无效——在 s=0.30（30% heads + 30% KV pruned）下仍然如此。这在计算上几乎不可能是自然结果（浮点非确定性至少应产生 ~1e-7 偏差）。\n\n**最可能原因**：importance-based masking 在 GSM8K 短序列上表现退化——importance ranking 将所有 globally unimportant heads/KV 集中在 non-low-sparsity positions，导致 low-sparsity phase 完全未被 mask 触及。但精确 0.0 暗示可能存在代码级问题（如 compute_dense_forward_with_importance 的 importance 矩阵在某些条件下返回的分布过于集中）。\n\n**2. [severity: critical] DS importance PASS verdict 是假阳性**\n\nCI overlap test 通过是因为 II_low CI = [0, 0]（退化数据）与 II_mid CI = [28.35, 570.06] 无交叠。这不是 phase modulation 的证据——任何常数（包括 0）与非零 CI 都不会交叠。verdict 算法未检测退化数据。\n\n**3. [severity: warning] DS random FAIL — phase modulation 弱于 MATH-500**\n\nDS random verdict = FAIL：low vs mid CI 在 s=0.3 处交叠（low: [4.81, 15.13], mid: [3.15, 23.93]）。虽然两者均显著大于 0，但无法证明 phase 间差异。这可能是 GSM8K 短 trace 的真实特性（phase differentiation 弱于 MATH-500）。\n\n**4. [severity: warning] 全部 aggregate perplexity = NaN**\n\n两个 DS 条件的 profiling_results.json 中，所有 phase × sparsity 的 dense/attn/kv/both perplexity 均为 NaN。per-problem II 从 per-segment PPL ratio 计算，aggregate PPL 是单独汇总逻辑，两者独立。aggregate PPL = NaN 是报告层的 bug，不影响 II 计算，但说明 GSM8K profiling 代码未充分测试。\n\n**5. [severity: warning] Mid-sparsity II 异常大**\n\nDS importance mid_sparsity II = 275（s025）、291（s030）——比 MATH-500 同条件大一个数量级。CI 极宽（[19, 654] 和 [28, 570]），说明受极端 outlier 驱动。bootstrap N=1000（GSM8K 用）vs N=10000（MATH-500）也降低了 CI 可靠性。\n\n**6. [severity: info] DS 两条件完成，QW 仍在运行**\n\n- DS importance: 50/50，输出已生成（profiling_results.json + generations.json）\n- DS random: 50/50，输出已生成\n- QW importance: 50/50 问题已处理，但输出目录为空（可能仍在聚合或崩溃）\n- QW random: 38/50，仍在运行\n- GPU 状态：GPU 0 idle（DS 完成），GPU 1/2/3 活跃\n\n### 建议\n\n1. **DS importance 结果不可用**——II_low = 0 的退化使 D_med 和 G_med 无法有意义地计算。不应在论文中引用此条件的 GSM8K 数据。\n2. **诊断必须项**：在 n=3 的小规模 diagnostic run 中，对 low-sparsity phase 的每个 segment 打印 ppl_dense/ppl_attn/ppl_kv/ppl_both 的具体数值，确认是否 masking 确实无效还是代码 bug。\n3. **DS random FAIL 的解读**：如果 QW 也 FAIL，可以诚实报告 \"GSM8K 上 phase modulation signal 弱于 MATH-500\"——这不否定主论文结论，但 cross-dataset claim 需要降级（从 \"generalizes\" 到 \"partially replicates\" 或放进 appendix 作为 limitation）。\n4. **等 QW 结果**：QW importance 可能提供对比数据（如果 QW importance ii_low 非零，则证明这是 DS-specific 而非 code-level bug）。\n5. **Bootstrap N 应匹配 MATH-500**：GSM8K 用 N=1000 vs MATH-500 用 N=10000，应统一。\n\n### GPU 资源检查\n\nnvidia-smi 确认 GPU 0 idle（DS 完成后释放），GPU 1/2/3 活跃。DS 运行在 GPU 0（importance）和 GPU 1（random），QW 运行在 GPU 2（importance）和 GPU 3（random），均在 execution.gpu_ids 白名单内（项目配置为全部 4 张卡）。无越界。"
}

### Assistant

_source line 1168 · 2026-05-15T16:48:15.681Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
The worker's conclusion is that:
1. DS importance: 50/50 complete, Verdict PASS, low_sparsity II≈0 is "as expected"
2. DS random: 50/50 complete, Verdict FAIL, random masking can't differentiate phases
3. The FAIL was a cron false alarm — processes exited normally but cron saw "process dead" without checking exit code
4. Registry updated to DONE/success

I need to critically review this. The worker claims "low_sparsity II≈0 符合预期" (as expected), but this is WRONG based on my investigation:


The II_low values are exactly 0.0 across all 100 non-null entries at every sparsity level—not approximately zero, but computationally degenerate. Meanwhile, MATH-500 DS importance shows rich non-zero II_low values ranging from -5.4 to 1524, directly contradicting the claim that zero values are expected for importance masking. The PASS verdict appears to be passing only because of this degeneracy, not because the test actually validates anything meaningful.

Beyond that, there are several critical issues the worker completely overlooked: all aggregate perplexity values are NaN, 50% of per-problem ii_low entries are null, and mid-sparsity II values are extreme (275, 291)—an order of magnitude larger than the MATH-500 equivalents. The worker marked this as DONE/success despite these red flags, which is a serious oversight.

On the cron false alarm claim, the worker's reasoning doesn't hold up either. The registry entry `gsm8k_profiling_ds` was configured to monitor DS random's verdict, and DS random's verdict is FAIL, so the cron should have correctly flagged the experiment as failed.

Given that DS random has a FAIL verdict and DS importance has degenerate data with all-zero II_low values, these results fundamentally don't support the cross-dataset validation claims. I need to escalate this as a critical review to the Director.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the MATH-500 DS importance results from registry for comparison:

MATH-500 DS importance (n=496 effective):
- imp_s015: median_ii = 0.3907, CI [0.3496, 0.4431], p = 1.27e-70
- imp_s020: median_ii = 1.2149, CI [1.1072, 1.3365], p = 3.98e-75


- imp_s025: median_ii = 3.9857, CI [3.5272, 4.3588], p = 1.99e-78
- imp_s030: median_ii = 8.4813, CI [7.7543, 9.6919], p = 1.09e-79

These D statistic values show the median differences between II_low and II_high. For GSM8K, the pattern is quite different—II_low is consistently 0 across non-null entries while II_high has a mean of -0.66, suggesting a different importance structure than MATH-500.

Looking at the specific II values across sparsity levels, I'm seeing II_high values like -0.02 and -2.48 at lower sparsity, then II_mid jumps dramatically to 275.54 and 291.36 at higher sparsity. This is fundamentally inconsistent with the MATH-500 results, and combined with the degenerate II_low values, NaN aggregate perplexity, and the random failure verdict on the DS check, the worker's claim of success doesn't hold up—the data structure itself contradicts what we'd expect. The pattern is anomalous and the conclusion feels premature. I'm also noting that GSM8K uses fewer bootstrap iterations than MATH-500, though that doesn't account for the zero values. I need to flag this as a critical issue in my review—the II_low degeneracy across all entries is suspicious, especially when compared to MATH-500's rich distribution, and the worker's claim of expected behavior doesn't hold up without proper baseline comparison. This could indicate either a bug in how importance masking interacts with GSM8K's shorter traces, or a genuine but unexplained cross-dataset difference that would undermine the generalization claims.

The PASS verdict itself is problematic since the CI overlap test trivially succeeds with [0,0] against mid-sparsity ranges—that's not evidence of phase modulation, just degenerate data. I'm also seeing that all perplexity aggregates are NaN across both conditions, which the worker never mentioned, and the 50% null rate for II_low means the effective sample size for low-sparsity analysis drops to around 25 per level. The mid-sparsity values are showing extreme outliers that need investigation.

Looking at the DS importance scores, the mid-sparsity means are 275.5 and 291.4—orders of magnitude larger than MATH-500 equivalents—which suggests the means are being driven by outliers rather than representing the actual distribution. The GSM8K script uses mean-based confidence intervals instead of the median-based approach MATH-500 uses, so these aren't reliable. The DS random FAIL verdict was correctly identified but the registry still got marked as success, which contradicts the MATH-500 finding where DS random showed weak positive phase differentiation. Given all these data quality issues, updating the registry to DONE/success is premature—the experiment ran without crashing, but the results aren't actually valid.

Before using these GSM8K results in the paper, I need to investigate the II_low anomaly with diagnostic logging, switch to median-based confidence intervals to match MATH-500, fix the aggregate perplexity NaN bug, and evaluate whether n=50 is even sufficient or if GSM8K needs n=100+ to reduce the null rate.
```

  本项目特定的不可违反约束（GPU 设备号、服务器名、账号、路径限制等）。
  写本项目特定的，不要抄 CLAUDE.md 通用 L1 红线。

### 2. 🖥️ 基础设施快照
  - 服务器名 + 工作目录 + Python 路径 + conda env

  格式 "条件 X 满足 → 动作 Y"。例如:
  - "Falcon-H1R 下载完成 → sed 修 MODEL_PATH → dry-run → 提交"
  - "pilot_011 出 GC4 结果 → ≥2pp 转架构 narrative，<2pp 写 null-result"

### 5. 🎯 战略上下文
  - 项目核心结论 / 决策链（D001..）
  - 下一个"你"最容易判断错的事 / 容易踩的坑

- 第 1 节必须是项目特定的（不是通用 L1 红线）
- 第 2 节让下一轮 BOOT 后能立即开工，避免重新 SSH 探查
